# Cleaned Up Notes

# Medieval Women’s Religious Writing: Europe and Beyond  
### Dr Ayoush Lazikani — Oxford Prelims Paper 2 / FHS 2 (2025)

---

## Introduction: Reading the Female Sacred  

Dr Ayoush Lazikani situates medieval women’s devotional writing within **a global history of religious imagination**.  
The lecture proceeds in three movements:  

1. **Julian of Norwich and Margery Kempe** — English exemplars of visionary writing.  
2. **Continental mystics** — Marie d’Oignies, Lutgard of Aywières, Gertrude the Great, Catherine of Siena, Teresa of Ávila.  
3. **Comparative perspectives** — Islamic Sufis and Hindu Bhakti poets such as Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya, Lallā Ded, and Mīrābāī:contentReference[oaicite:1]{index=1}.  

Her aim is not encyclopaedic coverage but a **“cartography of devotion”**: tracing resonances between women separated by geography, language, and creed.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Gender of Vision**  
> Women’s visionary writing arises within patriarchal structures that restrict authority to men, yet these visions **authorise their speakers**. Ecstasy becomes epistemology: bodily feeling generates theological knowledge.  

---

## I. Julian of Norwich (1343– c.1416) — Love as Meaning  

### The Anchorite and Her Revelations  

Julian, the first woman known to have composed a book in English, wrote *Revelations of Divine Love* (c.1373).  
At thirty she fell gravely ill and, believing herself near death, received **sixteen visions** of Christ’s passion, compassion, and indwelling love:contentReference[oaicite:2]{index=2}.  

She later expanded her *Short Text* into a *Long Text* after twenty years of meditation — a rare case of a medieval woman revising her own theology.  

Her central claim:  

> “Love was His meaning.”  

God’s nature is **boundless and maternal love**. Julian re-imagines the Trinity as an endlessly generative motherhood:  
Christ labours to bring humanity to birth and nurses it with his blood.  

> **Mini-Essay — Maternal Theology**  
> By naming Christ “Mother,” Julian collapses masculine hierarchy. Her language of gestation and nursing fuses incarnation and creation: divine labour replaces patriarchal law.  

### Sin and Vision  

Julian insists she never “saw” sin itself — only its wounds. Evil lacks substance; it is privation. Her optimism (“All shall be well”) arises from a vision of **cosmic indwelling**: the universe seen as a hazelnut resting in God’s palm.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Hazelnut World**  
> The tiny nut symbolises the finite held within the infinite. Julian’s theology of scale anticipates ecological thought: creation as fragile yet beloved matter.  

---

## II. Margery Kempe (c.1373–after 1438) — The Body in Tears  

A lay woman from King’s Lynn, mother of fourteen, and businesswoman, Margery dictated her *Book* to two scribes.  
It is both spiritual autobiography and travelogue: pilgrimage to Jerusalem, Rome, and Norwich — where she consulted Julian:contentReference[oaicite:3]{index=3}.  

Margery’s faith is performed through her body. She weeps uncontrollably at the sight of the crucifix or any violence, and her “boisterous weeping” earns public ridicule and charges of heresy.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Devotional Body**  
> Tears are Margery’s text. In an age that denied women learning, she writes in water what men wrote in ink. Her sob translates vision into sound — a sacramental language of feeling.  

In one episode she sees a mother nursing her child and bursts into tears, recalling Mary and the infant Christ. Every maternal scene becomes an icon.  
Her meeting with Julian anchors her authority: the older anchorite blesses her tears as “the gift of God.”  

---

## III. Continental Mystics: Europe Beyond England  

### Marie d’Oignies (1177–1213) and the Beguines  

In the Low Countries, groups of lay women called **Beguines** lived devoutly without taking vows.  
Marie d’Oignies, from Liège, is described by her confessor Jacques de Vitry as weeping so profusely that “the ground became muddy.”:contentReference[oaicite:4]{index=4}  
Her tears infect others: the priest who rebukes her for crying is himself overwhelmed by tears at Mass.  

> **Mini-Essay — Contagious Devotion**  
> Emotion is communal and performative. Marie's weeping creates a liturgical circle in which feeling itself is the Eucharist.  

### Lutgard of Aywières (1182–1246)  

A Cistercian nun who experienced visions of drinking from Christ’s side wound:   
> “He embraced her and pressed her mouth to the wound in His right side.”:contentReference[oaicite:5]{index=5}  

Erotic and eucharistic imagery merge: union through wound, nourishment through blood.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Erotics of Wounding**  
> The body of Christ becomes both lover and food. Medieval female mysticism renders eros a path to communion, where pain is transfigured into intimacy.  

Lutgard’s vision of Pope Innocent III in purgatory is politically charged: divine justice equalises gender and rank.  

---

## IV. Gertrude the Great (1256–1302) — The Heart as Womb  

Author of *The Herald of Divine Love*, Gertrude developed the cult of the **Sacred Heart**.  
She describes Christ imprinting His wounds upon her heart:  

> “Thou hadst imprinted in the depths of my heart the adorable marks of Thy sacred wounds.”:contentReference[oaicite:6]{index=6}  

For Gertrude, Christ’s heart beats within her own; she hears its pulse as speech.  
Alexandra Barratt likens the heart to a **maternal space**, where the devotee returns to the rhythm of the divine womb.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Heart as Habitation**  
> Gertrude makes indwelling literal: devotion as cardiac architecture. The heart is both cradle and cathedral of the Word.  

---

## V. Catherine of Siena (1347–1380) — The Political Mystic  

An Italian Dominican tertiary, Catherine experienced visions of **mystical marriage** to Christ, receiving a ring from the Virgin.  
Her letters — nearly 400 to popes, princes, and lay people — reveal a woman who used visionary authority to influence diplomacy:contentReference[oaicite:7]{index=7}.  

During the War of the Eight Saints she sought to reconcile Florence and the papacy.  
Her politics derive from mysticism: union with Christ becomes a model for social unity.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Politics of Marriage**  
> Catherine’s “mystical marriage” translates private eros into public ethics. The spouse of Christ advises popes — a gendered reversal of authority.  

---

## VI. Teresa of Ávila (1515–1582) — Pain as Ecstasy  

In her *Life*, Teresa describes a vision of an angel piercing her heart with a flaming spear:  

> “He plunged it into my heart several times so that it penetrated to my entrails… The pain was so sweet that I would not wish it to cease.”:contentReference[oaicite:8]{index=8}  

Gian Lorenzo Bernini’s *Ecstasy of Saint Teresa* (1652) visualises this moment in baroque marble: bodily surrender as divine rapture.  

Teresa’s visions of hell also display psychological depth: a furnace of “filthy wet mud” and “wicked reptiles” more terrifying than physical pain.  
Hell is not only place but state of mind — separation from God.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Ambivalence of Pleasure**  
> Teresa’s spear-wound and her vision of hell mirror each other: both are penetrations of the soul. Pain and bliss become indistinguishable signs of divine intimacy.  

---

## VII. Beyond Europe: Comparative Mysticisms  

### Lallā Ded (14th c., Kashmir)  

A Shaivite poet-saint, Lallā Ded (“Lal Dād”) wrote short Kashmiri vakhs (poems) celebrating the God Śiva as indwelling spirit:  

> “You hid from me all day, and when I found you hiding inside me I ran wild — now me, now you.”:contentReference[oaicite:9]{index=9}  

Her poetry echoes Julian’s doctrine of indwelling love and Gertrude’s heart mysticism.  

> **Mini-Essay — Interior Divinity**  
> The cross-cultural resonance is not in doctrine but in experience: Lallā and Julian both locate God within, making the soul its own monastery.  

### Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya (8th c., Basra)  

The Sufi poet Rābiʿa distinguished two loves of God:  

> “I love You with two loves: a love of passion and a love that is due to You.”:contentReference[oaicite:10]{index=10}  

Her poem anticipates Julian’s dual love of desire and grace. Both use the language of eros to purify it into agapē.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Two Loves**  
> Passion is not inferior but gateway to vision. Rābiʿa’s erotic imagery redefines holiness as longing without possession — a love that needs no reward.  

### Mīrābāī (16th c., Rajasthan)  

A princess who renounced royalty to sing for Krishna, Mīrābāī composed songs of devotional friendship.  
Modern iconography shows her dancing before Krishna — the body as instrument of praise.  

> **Mini-Essay — Friendship as Theology**  
> Both Mīrābāī and Margery Kempe model devotion through affectionate companionship rather than hierarchy. Lazikani, following Alexandra Ferrini, calls this “the mysticism of friendship” — cross-cultural empathy as spiritual method.  

---

## Conclusion: The Global Mystic and the Scholar’s Task  

Dr Lazikani ends with a call for **comparative reading as ethical practice**.  
To place Julian beside Rābiʿa, or Margery beside Mīrābāī, is not to erase difference but to **listen across traditions**.  

> **Mini-Essay — Devotion as Dialogue**  
> Comparative mysticism reveals how cultures meet in attention, not assimilation. Each visionary teaches the same method: to see the divine in the other, and the other in the divine.

---

## Motif Tracker  

| **Motif** | **Example(s)** | **Interpretive Use** |
|------------|----------------|----------------------|
| **Maternal Divinity** | Julian’s “Christ as Mother”; Gertrude’s Sacred Heart | The divine reimagined through gestation, feeding, and care; motherhood as model of creation. |
| **Tears and Bodily Devotion** | Margery Kempe; Marie d’Oignies | Physical emotion as text; the body writes what the intellect cannot. |
| **Erotic Mysticism** | Lutgard’s side wound; Teresa’s spear vision | Union with God figured through bodily desire; eros transfigured into communion. |
| **Heart Theology** | Gertrude’s *Herald of Divine Love* | The heart as site of revelation and dwelling; devotion internalised as rhythm. |
| **Authority through Vision** | Julian’s revelations; Catherine’s letters | Vision as licence for female speech; the mystical replaces scholastic authority. |
| **The Politics of Piety** | Catherine mediating papal politics | Mystical intimacy empowering public voice; private revelation turned diplomacy. |
| **Pain and Sweetness** | Teresa’s “pain so sweet”; Julian’s “all shall be well” | Paradox of suffering as joy; affect as spiritual knowledge. |
| **Friendship and Companionship** | Mīrābāī’s songs; Margery’s pilgrims | Devotion imagined as shared intimacy rather than hierarchy; love as equality. |
| **Interior Divinity** | Lallā Ded; Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya | Global resonance of God-within; mystical self as microcosm of the divine. |
| **Comparative Mysticism** | Julian ↔ Lallā Ded; Margery ↔ Mīrābāī | Cross-cultural empathy: seeing the same divine light refracted through different languages. |

---

## Critical Lenses  

| **Axis** | **Relationship** | **Implication** |
|-----------|------------------|----------------|
| **Body ↔ Spirit** | Tears, wounds, and ecstasy | Physical sensation as conduit to revelation; holiness felt rather than abstracted. |
| **Gender ↔ Authority** | Female visionaries’ speech | Mystical experience grants women rhetorical and theological power otherwise denied. |
| **Private ↔ Public** | Contemplation and politics | Mysticism as activism; inner transformation reshapes outer order. |
| **Pain ↔ Pleasure** | Teresa’s “sweet pain” | Affective paradox; divine love transcends moral binaries. |
| **Text ↔ Performance** | Dictation, tears, pilgrimage | Writing as embodied act; emotion as authorship. |
| **Local ↔ Global** | England and the wider world | Comparative reading reimagines medieval literature as transnational. |
| **Silence ↔ Speech** | Julian’s secrecy, Margery’s outcry | Voice negotiated through obedience and excess; speech as revelation. |
| **Friendship ↔ Hierarchy** | Mīrābāī’s Krishna songs | Love as equalising force; spiritual democracy across faiths. |

---

## Exam Relevance  

### Core Relevance to Prelims / FHS Paper 2  

- Broadens the category of **medieval literature** to include women’s visionary prose and global devotional poetry.  
- Demonstrates how religious writing engages questions of **gender, language, and embodiment**.  
- Introduces **cross-cultural comparison** as critical method — linking English, Continental, Islamic, and Indic traditions.  
- Encourages nuanced understanding of **authority and authorship** in pre-modern women’s voices.  

### Analytical Skills  

- Close reading of visionary language: syntax of revelation, metaphors of motherhood, pain, and interiority.  
- Recognising **genre hybridity** — autobiography, theology, lyric, and travel narrative.  
- Integrating **material culture and art** (manuscripts, sculpture, Bernini’s *Ecstasy of St Teresa*).  
- Applying theoretical lenses (feminist, postcolonial, comparative theology).  

### Model Essay Patterns  

1. **“Love was His meaning.”** — Examine how medieval women use affect and metaphor to articulate theology.  
2. **“The body is the page.”** — Discuss embodiment as authorship in Margery Kempe and Teresa of Ávila.  
3. **“Mysticism is politics.”** — Analyse Catherine of Siena and Lallā Ded as public theologians.  
4. **“Across traditions, the language of desire becomes the language of God.”** — Compare Julian with Rābiʿa or Mīrābāī.  

### Revision Strategies  

- Learn key visions (Julian’s hazelnut, Teresa’s spear, Lutgard’s wound) and their theological meanings.  
- Trace the **lexicon of love** — “sweetness,” “wound,” “mother,” “friendship.”  
- Compare **translation and mediation**: many texts exist through male scribes or hagiographers.  
- Reflect on **methodological humility** — how modern reading risks domesticating radical experience.  

---

## Reference List (MHRA Style)  

Barratt, Alexandra, *Women’s Writing in Middle English* (London: Routledge, 1992).  
Bernini, Gian Lorenzo, *The Ecstasy of Saint Teresa*, marble sculpture, Cornaro Chapel, Rome (1647–52).

Teresa of Ávila, *The Life of Teresa of Jesus*, trans. E. Allison Peers (London: Sheed & Ward, 1946).

---

# Cleaned Up Notes

## What is Literature?  
### Oxford Prelims Paper 1B / Paper 3 Core Lecture  

---

## Introduction: The Question that Refuses an Answer  

Few questions in literary study are more persistent — or more productive — than “What is literature?” Every attempt to define it seems to dissolve into new questions about language, value, imagination, and power. As the lecturer remarks, “It’s hard to imagine a definition of literature that wouldn’t find room for *To be or not to be.* But why?”:contentReference[oaicite:0]{index=0}  

This lecture moves from Shakespeare to Wordsworth, from Beckett to William Carlos Williams, to show how definitions of literature are themselves acts of criticism — each definition is also a kind of reading.

> **Mini-Essay — The Question of Literature**
>
> To ask “what is literature?” is to perform a literary act: to interpret, to weigh form and meaning, to create connections between texts. The category “literature” is not fixed; it is the record of how cultures imagine the power and limits of language itself.

---

## I. Hamlet and the Problem of Definition  

The lecture begins, fittingly, with *Hamlet*’s “To be or not to be,” a speech so famous it defines literature even as it resists definition. Why do we read this soliloquy as literature, rather than as “the theatrical equivalent of a teenager howling, ‘God, I wish I was dead’”?:contentReference[oaicite:1]{index=1}  

Possible answers:  

- **Form:** iambic pentameter that “topples over” with extra syllables — a pattern of control and loss.  
- **Patterning and Repetition:** the play’s obsession with questioning (“question it, Horatio”).  
- **Cultural Longevity:** its echo through later works — Eliot’s *Prufrock*, cartoons, film, even advertising.  
- **Ethical Reflection:** Hamlet’s “or” models balance and tolerance — a miniature of moral deliberation.  
- **Language as Renewal:** “sea of troubles” was a cliché, yet Shakespeare “rinses it clean.”  

Each feature is plausible, yet none singly defines literature. As the lecture notes, *Hamlet*’s artistry depends not on one property, but on the **family resemblance** among many.

> **Mini-Essay — The Ethics of Ambiguity**  
> Hamlet’s soliloquy enacts literature’s defining gesture: to pose a problem without resolving it. Its rhythm of questioning — “to be or not to be” — becomes a structure of ethical imagination. The undecidable “or” marks literature’s refusal of certainty, its balance between action and reflection.

---

## II. Aesthetic Form and Ethical Value  

### Literature and the Moral Imagination  

From the Romantics to modern critics such as Martha Nussbaum, literature has been celebrated as a **training ground for empathy**. Reading fiction “allows us to feel our way into the lives of others.” George Eliot’s *Middlemarch* does not merely *depict* society; it asks us to *recreate* it imaginatively.  

Yet the lecture warns against sentimental assumptions: aesthetically fine books do not automatically produce moral readers. Stalin, after all, annotated hundreds of books daily, scrawling “rubbish” or “haha” in the margins. “A reader who failed to see that it wasn’t only when opening a work of fiction he held other people’s lives in his hands”:contentReference[oaicite:2]{index=2}.  

> **Mini-Essay — Reading and Responsibility**  
> Literature exercises empathy, but it does not guarantee virtue. The reader’s ethical test is not emotional sympathy but interpretive attention — the willingness to recognise language as action, not ornament.

---

## III. Making It New: Literature as Renewal  

Ezra Pound’s dictum “Make it new” describes literature’s task as linguistic resurrection. Samuel Beckett’s *How It Is* (“that’s not said any more it must have ceased to be of interest”) performs this renewal by breaking syntax to rediscover speech. Yet, as the lecture wryly notes, renewal is also what headlines and football chants do: “Super Caley go ballistic Celtic are atrocious.”  

Thus, form alone cannot define literature; novelty can appear in the most banal places. The power of *Hamlet* or Beckett lies not simply in originality but in the way **form reawakens thought**.

---

## IV. What the Dictionaries Don’t Tell Us  

The Oxford English Dictionary’s entry for *literature* lists everything from “the realm of letters” to “written work valued for superior or lasting artistic merit.” This breadth exposes the problem: to define literature is to **draw boundaries around a moving target**.  

### Terry Eagleton and Family Resemblance  

In *The Event of Literature* (2012), Eagleton argues that literary definition works less by strict criteria than by *family resemblances* — a concept from Wittgenstein. Works may share traits (fictionality, formal pattern, imaginative depth), yet none is necessary or sufficient on its own.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Family of Words**  
> Literature resembles a family portrait: no single feature defines kinship, but resemblance appears across generations. As with families, resemblance also includes rivalry — a constant rewriting of lineage.

Raymond Williams traced the word’s shifting meaning:  
- **14th century:** “polite learning” — literacy as social polish.  
- **Renaissance:** shared knowledge — Bacon’s “erudition, divine and humane.”  
- **Romantics:** creativity and imagination — the “secondary world.”  
By the 19th century, literature meant **imaginative writing**; by the 21st, it risks meaning **everything**.

---

## V. Literature and Power: Canon, Culture, and Critique  

### From De Quincey to the Canon Wars  

Thomas De Quincey distinguished “the literature of knowledge” (newspapers, reports) from “the literature of power” (Milton, Shakespeare). Later critics — Arnold, Leavis — saw literature as moral education. But postmodern critics argue that *“literature” is an evaluative label*, used to legitimise cultural hierarchies.  

Roland Barthes’s provocation (1969): *“Literature is what gets taught.”*  

### Canon Debates  
The 1980s “canon wars” questioned whose experience literature represents. Should “great books” courses, filled with “dead white European males,” represent the diversity of living readers?  

Frank Kermode’s *History and Value* (1988) offered a conciliatory view: the canon is “cultural memory,” capable of expansion without destruction.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Politics of Inclusion**  
> Every canon is a mirror: it reflects not only what a culture values but what it excludes. Expanding the canon is not about replacing works but rethinking the principles of inheritance.

---

## VI. Language, Use, and the Literary Function  

Does literature use language in a “special” way? The lecture suggests two answers.  

### 1. **Defamiliarisation** — The Russian Formalists  
Viktor Shklovsky’s 1917 essay “Art as Technique” argued that art makes the familiar strange: it “defamiliarises” the world, forcing us to see anew. William Carlos Williams’s *This Is Just to Say* performs this by turning a domestic note into a poem — an act of everyday estrangement.  

### 2. **Interpretive Communities** — Stanley Fish  
Fish’s famous classroom experiment (*Is There a Text in This Class?*, 1980) revealed that meaning depends not on textual properties but on **the expectations of readers**. A list of names becomes a “religious poem” once readers believe it is one.  

> **Mini-Essay — Literature as Agreement**  
> Literature is not a property of language but of *attention*. The literary arises when readers choose to read as if something were literature. The poem, the play, the shopping list — all depend on the interpretive act.

---

## VII. The Boundaries of Literature: From Kipling to Culture  

Rudyard **Kipling’s** imperial poem “The Burial” (1902) memorialises Cecil Rhodes as a conquering spirit whose “soul shall be her soul.” Should it remain in the canon? Walter Benjamin’s dictum — “There is no document of civilisation which is not at the same time a document of barbarism” — warns that aesthetic monuments are also political ones.  

Christopher Ricks offers a subtler solution: later writers decide which predecessors survive, by rewriting or refuting them. T. S. Eliot’s modernism keeps the metaphysical poets alive precisely by transforming them.  

Thus, literature is not a static inheritance but a **conversation across time** — a sequence of reanimations.

---

## VIII. Work and Play: Two Modes of Literature  

Drawing on Seamus Heaney’s *The Redress of Poetry*, the lecture concludes with a twin model.  

### 1. Literature as Work  
Literature **does things**: it acts, persuades, and reshapes ideology. Like a delayed “speech act,” it changes the world indirectly — *Uncle Tom’s Cabin* inspiring abolition, Goethe’s *Werther* provoking suicides. For Marxist critics like Sartre or Macherey, literature reveals hidden structures of power.  

### 2. Literature as Play  
Tom Stoppard said, “Art celebrates a world which includes itself.” Literature as play is language freed from duty — “language going on holiday,” in Wittgenstein’s phrase. From Lear’s nonsense to Joyce’s *Finnegans Wake*, such writing re-enchants the medium itself.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Twin Life of Art**  
> The most enduring works do both. Wordsworth’s *“I wandered lonely as a cloud”* is both reflective work and joyous play: a labour of imagination that turns recollection into dancing movement. Its “crowd… host of golden daffodils” is both scene and self-reflection — a mind working and playing at once.

---

## IX. The Ongoing Conversation  

The lecture closes by noting that literature’s definition is always unfinished. Every new work redefines the field — just as every reader redefines what counts as reading.  

> **Mini-Essay — Literature as Process**  
> Literature is not an object but a conversation — between words and worlds, readers and writers, work and play. It is what keeps language alive, and what keeps us answerable to language.  

---

## Motif Tracker  

| **Motif** | **Example(s)** | **Interpretive Use** |
|------------|----------------|----------------------|
| **Allusion and Echo** | Hamlet → Prufrock; Beckett → Shakespeare | Literature as repetition-with-difference. |
| **Family Resemblance** | Eagleton via Wittgenstein | Literature as network, not essence. |
| **Canon / Power** | Barthes: “what gets taught” | Institutions shape the literary. |
| **Defamiliarisation** | Shklovsky; Williams’s *This Is Just to Say* | Art as renewal of perception. |
| **Interpretive Community** | Stanley Fish’s “Jacobs-Rosenbaum” | Reading as social act, not solitary discovery. |
| **Work and Play** | Sartre vs. Stoppard | Dual nature of literature’s function. |

---

## Critical Lenses  

| **Axis** | **Relationship** | **Interpretive Implication** |
|-----------|------------------|-------------------------------|
| **Imagination ↔ Morality** | Nussbaum, Eliot, Heaney | Reading as ethical rehearsal. |
| **Form ↔ Function** | Shklovsky, Williams | Poetic form transforms ordinary language. |
| **Tradition ↔ Revision** | Ricks, Eliot | Writers keep the canon alive through dialogue. |
| **Culture ↔ Politics** | Barthes, Benjamin | Literature as site of power and critique. |
| **Reader ↔ Text** | Fish, Eagleton | Meaning as negotiated community act. |

---

## Exam Relevance  

### For Oxford Prelims Paper 1B and Paper 3  
- Introduces key critical movements (Formalism, Reader-Response, Marxist and Ethical criticism).  
- Builds foundation for analysing **form, value, and cultural context**.  
- Provides frameworks for essay questions on the definition, function, and ethics of literature.  

### Model Essay Patterns  
1. **“Defining Literature: From Family Resemblance to Interpretive Community.”**  
2. **“Work and Play: The Two Lives of Language.”**  
3. **“Canon and Culture: Who Decides What Literature Is?”**  

### Revision Strategies  
- Compare passages (*Hamlet*, *This Is Just to Say*, *Daffodils*) for different models of literary language.  
- Trace critical genealogies — from De Quincey’s “literature of power” to Eagleton’s “event.”  
- Practise defining literature in a single sentence — then rewrite it five different ways.  

---

# Cleaned Up Notes

## Literature and History: Context, Temporality, and Reading Across Time  
### Oxford Prelims Paper 1B – Core Lecture  

---

## Introduction: The Problem of Context  

Professor **Joe Moshenska** begins with a thought experiment: imagine that one of your own writings — a diary, an essay, even a note — is discovered centuries in the future. Scholars of that future, desperate for traces of the early twenty-first century, pore over every sentence as evidence of its age.  

But what would count as your “context”? Your education, gender, language? The climate crisis? The internet age? Or things you yourself never noticed shaping your thought? (Moshenska 2025).

> **Mini-Essay — The Mirage of Context**  
> “Context” feels like a bridge between text and history, but it often hides what it claims to reveal. To *put* a work “in context” assumes that the text is inert, waiting to be contained by its background. Moshenska’s thought experiment exposes the fiction of containment: context is not a tub for margarine, but a web of invisible forces — many of which the writer never perceives (Moshenska 2025).

### The Double Meaning of “Context”  

The Oxford English Dictionary traces *context* to the Latin *contextus* — “that which is woven together.” Originally, it referred to **the relations within a text**, not its surroundings. Over time, the word’s sense flipped outward: from the inner weave of a text to the external network around it. Literature, however, still operates in both senses — as a tissue of interrelations within itself and with history.

> **Mini-Essay — The Text as Its Own Context**  
> Every work of art is both container and contained. The texture of its language, its repetitions, and silences form a history of their own — not commentary upon the world but sediment of it. To read contextually, then, is also to read **the history within form** (Felski 2015).

---

## I. Scales of Context  

Moshenska asks: how large or small can context be?  
- **Micro**: biographical details — age, gender, class.  
- **Meso**: institutions — the university, the city, the nation.  
- **Macro**: historical epochs — capitalism, empire, “the internet age.”  

A writer may be unaware of these frameworks, yet they structure her imagination. Contexts are always **multiple** and **partly unconscious**. The author is not necessarily “the best arbiter” of her own contexts (Jameson 1981).

---

## II. Always Historicise! — Fredric Jameson  

Fredric Jameson’s dictum from *The Political Unconscious* (1981) provides a rallying cry: **“Always historicise!”** Yet, as Jameson argues, historicising can proceed along **two paths**: (Jameson 1981):  
1. **The path of the object** — the historical origins of a text’s forms and contents.  
2. **The path of the subject** — the historical origins of our interpretive categories themselves.  

The second path radicalises the first: *our very ways of reading are historical.* Thought itself has a history.

> **Mini-Essay — The Historicity of Understanding**  
> To read historically is not merely to reconstruct a vanished world but to recognise that our own minds belong to history. Interpretation is itself an event in time — part of the story literature tells about how humans make meaning (Jameson 1981).

---

## III. From New Criticism to New Historicism  

### The Reaction Against Autonomy  

The **New Critics** (e.g. Cleanth Brooks, W.K. Wimsatt, R.P. Blackmur) treated poems as self-contained “well-wrought urns.” New Historicists rejected this autonomy, insisting that texts are **sites of social energy** rather than sealed aesthetic objects (Brooks 1947).

### Stephen Greenblatt and the “Circulation of Social Energy”  

Stephen Greenblatt’s work reframes literary texts as participants in cultural flows — political, religious, economic — a project most concentratedly argued in *Shakespearean Negotiations* (1988) and Practicing New Historicism (Greenblatt 1988; Gallagher & Greenblatt 2000).

His essay “Invisible Bullets” begins not with Shakespeare but with a spy’s note reporting Marlowe’s heresies; from there he links Elizabethan espionage, exploration, and theatrical politics in a series of associative readings (Greenblatt 1988).

> **Mini-Essay — The Anecdote as Method**  
> The anecdote embodies the New Historicist belief that history’s meaning is dispersed, not hierarchical. A court record, a travel diary, a play: all transmit the same “social energy.” The critic’s task is to trace these invisible currents rather than to prove causation (Gallagher & Greenblatt 2000).

### Limits of the Method  

Moshenska notes that this levelling of text and context sometimes re-established old hierarchies: canonical authors (Shakespeare above others) remained central, and broad claims about cultural “circulation” risk reproducing the periodisation New Historicism claimed to critique (Moshenska 2025; Greenblatt 1988).

---

## IV. Beyond the Period: Rethinking Temporality  

Recent theory unsettles the very notion of linear, unified time.

### Michel Serres — The Polychronic World  

Michel Serres describes historical eras as **multitemporal**; objects and texts gather together obsolete, contemporary, and future strands — a “folded” or pleated temporality that resists linear chronology (Serres 1995).

> “Every historical era is multitemporal, simultaneously drawing from the obsolete, the contemporary, and the futuristic… an object is thus polychronic, multitemporal, and reveals a time that is gathered together, with multiple pleats.” (Serres 1995)

Time, for Serres, is not a line but a **folded fabric**. Literature embodies this folding — carrying past and future within its present.

### Bruno Latour — We Have Never Been Modern  

Bruno Latour extends this insight in *We Have Never Been Modern* (1993): modern temporality is the product of a disciplinary separation that flattens heterogeneous times into a single historical frame. Once we recognise “quasi-objects” (hybrids of categories), the neat order of historical periodisation unravels (Latour 1993).

> **Mini-Essay — The Pleated Present**  
> To read a text as “of its time” is already to repress the anachronisms it carries. A poem is never modern or medieval alone; it folds many moments into one. The act of reading unfolds those pleats, releasing the future hidden in the past (Latour 1993; Serres 1995).

### Queer Temporalities  

Elizabeth Freeman’s *Time Binds* (2010) and Carolyn Dinshaw’s *How Soon Is Now?* (2007/2012) bring these temporal critiques into contact with queer theory: Freeman coins **chrononormativity** to name how social life is structured by normative temporal schedules (marriage, reproduction, career), while Dinshaw celebrates **queer anachronism** that refuses tidy historical sequencing (Freeman 2010; Dinshaw 2012).

These approaches make temporality itself a political and aesthetic question.

### Rita Felski — The Busy Afterlife of the Text  

Rita Felski has reminded critics that the literary artefact has a busy afterlife that repeatedly reactivates meanings and resists being neatly boxed into its original moment: “The busy afterlife of the literary artefact refutes our efforts to box it into a moment of origin” (Felski 2015).

> **Mini-Essay — The Text as Temporal Actor**  
> Literature does not merely belong to history; it *makes* history. Each reading reactivates the work’s potential energies. The poem survives not as residue of the past but as participant in the present — and architect of future meanings (Felski 2015; Freeman 2010).

---

## V. Case Study: John Donne’s *“The Relic”*  

To illustrate these theories, Moshenska turns to Donne’s *The Relic* (c.1611) — a poem obsessed with time, resurrection, and misrecognition.

> *“When my grave is broke up again, / Some second guest to entertain…”* (Donne, *The Relic*)

The speaker imagines his bones exhumed, mistaken for saintly relics. Donne’s conceit fuses the **earthly and eternal**, the **sacred and profane**. Historically, relics were contested in post-Reformation England; Donne, who converted from Anglicanism to Catholic-adjacent practices before his Anglican ordination, knew the religious stakes. A historicist reading is valuable, but the poem also stages a deeper **temporal experiment**.

Relics themselves are *objects out of time* — decayed matter imbued with new significance. Donne’s poem mimics this paradox: it survives as a relic of language, a body of words animated by successive readers (Donne c.1611).

> **Mini-Essay — The Poem as Relic**  
> *The Relic* enacts what it describes. It is unearthed by each generation, reinterpreted, mistaken, adored. Its temporality is “pleated”: future, present, and past intertwine. To read it is to take part in the miracle of its misrecognition (Donne c.1611; Serres 1995).

Donne’s later prose (*Devotions upon Emergent Occasions*, 1624) makes this temporal anxiety explicit: “Before you sound that word *present*, or that monosyllable *now*, the present and the now is past” (Donne 1624). For Donne, as for Serres, time itself is unstable — a vanishing instant.

---

## VI. Conclusion: Literature as Multitemporal Thought  

To “historicise” is not to trap a work in its past but to perceive its **movement through time**. A literary text is a **polychronic artefact**: it contains the residues of its origins, the transformations of its readers, and the futures it continues to invent.

> **Mini-Essay — Reading in Deep Time**  
> The act of reading is itself historical. When we study Donne, we are not returning to 1611 but creating a new intersection of moments — his, ours, and those to come. Literature and history are not separate disciplines but intertwined modes of thinking temporally (Jameson 1981; Serres 1995; Felski 2015).

---

## Motif Tracker  

| **Motif** | **Example(s)** | **Interpretive Use** |
|------------|----------------|----------------------|
| **Context / Text** | Milton’s notion of sacred textuality; modern theorists | Context as both internal texture and external network. |
| **Historicising** | Jameson’s “two paths” | Reading as historical action; the critic as subject of history. |
| **Social Energy** | Greenblatt’s *Shakespearean Negotiations* | Literature as participant in cultural power. |
| **Polychronic Time** | Serres, Latour, Donne | Texts as multi-temporal folds, not linear products. |
| **Chrononormativity** | Freeman, Dinshaw | Temporal order as social discipline; anachronism as resistance. |
| **Afterlife** | Felski’s “busy afterlife” | The text’s continued agency beyond its origin. |

---

## Critical Lenses  

| **Axis** | **Relationship** | **Implication** |
|-----------|------------------|----------------|
| **Text ↔ Context** | Interwoven, not hierarchical | Context exists inside the work (Felski 2015). |
| **Past ↔ Future** | Time as pleated, recursive | Literature creates as well as records history (Serres 1995). |
| **Form ↔ History** | Form carries historical consciousness | Style as historical document (Jameson 1981). |
| **Reader ↔ Text** | Reading as historical encounter | Every interpretation reconfigures temporality (Dinshaw 2012). |
| **Subject ↔ Object** | Both historically constituted | Criticism is itself a historical act (Gallagher & Greenblatt 2000). |

---

## Exam Relevance  

**For Paper 1B (Introduction to Literature and History):**  
- Develops conceptual precision about *context*, *periodisation*, and *temporality*.  
- Encourages critical reflection on what it means to “put a text in context.”  
- Provides frameworks for analysing historical theory (Jameson, Greenblatt, Serres, Latour, Felski).

### Model Essay Patterns  
1. **“Always historicise!”** — Discuss Jameson’s two paths of historicising and their implications for literary study (Jameson 1981).  
2. **“Every historical era is multitemporal.”** — Explore Serres and Latour through a reading of Donne’s *The Relic* (Serres 1995; Latour 1993; Donne c.1611).  
3. **“The busy afterlife of the literary artefact refutes our efforts to box it into a moment of origin.”** — Evaluate Felski’s claim using examples from premodern or modern texts (Felski 2015).

---

Dinshaw, Carolyn, *How Soon Is Now?* (Durham, NC: Duke University Press, 2012).  
Felski, Rita, *The Limits of Critique* (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2015).  
Freeman, Elizabeth, *Time Binds: Queer Temporalities, Queer Histories* (Durham, NC: Duke University Press, 2010).  
Gallagher, Catherine, and Stephen Greenblatt, *Practicing New Historicism* (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2000).  
Greenblatt, Stephen, *Shakespearean Negotiations* (Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1988).  
Jameson, Fredric, *The Political Unconscious* (London: Methuen, 1981).

# Cleaned Up Notes

## Measured Language: Metre and Meaning in English Poetry

---

## Introduction: Why Metre Matters

“**Metre means literally measured language**,” begins the lecture, drawing attention to the paradox at the heart of poetic form — that poetry measures something whose substance resists precise measurement (Dasgupta 2025). The Greek *metron* denotes “measure,” yet in English verse what is measured is not physical length but rhythm — the rise and fall of stress, the pulse of speech stylised into pattern. This tension between fixity and flux, between “measured language” and living speech, lies at the centre of poetic art.

Before the twentieth century, **metre was almost universal** in poetry written in English; to write without it was an experiment, not the norm. The patterned repetition of sound was seen as both a **moral and aesthetic principle** — a mode of beauty, discipline, and enchantment. Its very regularity made it **memorable and transmissible**, “a powerful restraint on textual corruption,” as Dante’s *terza rima* demonstrates, each rhyme interlocking like the gears of a mechanism designed to preserve sense (Dante 1949).

> **Mini-Essay — Metre as Memory**
>
> In societies where writing was unstable or rare, metre functioned as a kind of mnemonic technology — a “metrical alarm” against error. The pattern of rhythm became a safeguard, an audible signal of authenticity. To alter the pattern was to betray the poem’s integrity. This notion persists, subliminally, even in print culture: we “hear” when a line sounds wrong. Metre, then, is not mere ornament but a medium of truth, a moral architecture built from sound.

---

## Classical and Philosophical Origins

### Plato’s Quarrel with Poetry

Plato, in *Republic* X, dismissed poetry as “three removes from reality,” a copy of a copy, a seductively painted shadow. Yet his critique betrays awe as much as suspicion. He saw in **metrical language a power of enchantment** — “a brightly coloured web, a spell” capable of charming its hearers into false belief (Plato 1993).

Plato’s anxiety rests on metre’s dual nature: it **binds sound to sense**, but also separates them. The rhythmic allure of verse can seem to detach meaning from rational control, letting words “trifle on the ear” while bypassing the intellect. Yet in recognising this, Plato also identifies poetry’s cognitive force: its capacity to **make thought memorable by patterning it**.

> **Mini-Essay — From Plato to Sidney**
>
> The Platonic suspicion of rhythm as intoxication is countered by Sir Philip Sidney’s defence of poetry as ethical seduction: “Even as the child is often brought to take most wholesome things by hiding them in such other words as have a pleasant taste.” Metre becomes a **sweetened medicine**, where pleasure and instruction fuse. The poem’s measured form gives moral gravity to delight — not a distraction from truth, but its most persuasive form (Sidney 2002).

---

## Metre as a Cultural Technology

From Plato’s Greece to Wordsworth’s Lake District, poets have treated metre not as a decorative supplement but as a **mode of knowledge and transmission**. In Aristotle’s *Poetics*, verse is “more easily remembered than prose,” because “the measures of verse can be numbered” (Aristotle 1996). Measured language is thus also measurable thought — the intellect given rhythm.

Before literacy, metre served as a **structural memory aid**. Even after print, its regularity continued to function as a form of discipline. Dante’s *terza rima* not only organised narrative but **guarded against corruption**: a missing rhyme was an audible error. The form thus embodies a self-checking mechanism, an acoustic integrity.

> **Mini-Essay — Metre as Incorruptibility**
>
> Dante’s rhyme scheme (*aba bcb cdc …*) binds the poem’s progress to repetition and expectation. The ear enforces fidelity: to deviate is to produce dissonance. This idea of form as restraint challenges modern notions of artistic freedom. In the pre-modern imagination, metre does not imprison the poet; it **frees the poem from oblivion**. Its constraint guarantees survival, its discipline a form of permanence.

---

## What Is Stress? The Physics of Rhythm

To study metre is to study **stress** — the dynamic interplay of emphasis, pitch, and duration. English verse, unlike Latin or Greek, is built not on syllabic quantity but on the pattern of stressed and unstressed beats.

Linguists distinguish several types of stress:

1. **Lexical stress** — fixed within words (e.g. *blackbird* vs *black bird*).  
2. **Prosodic stress** — formed through syntax and emphasis within sentences.  
3. **Focal stress** — used to alter meaning through emphasis (*I went OUT* vs *I went out*).

Stress, therefore, is not a single acoustic property but a **composite effect** — involving loudness, pitch, and duration (Dasgupta 2025). In performance, stressed syllables are often slightly lengthened, louder, and higher in pitch, but none of these features alone defines them.

Old English verse organised its rhythms by **patterns of stress and pitch**, not by syllable count. Modern English preserves traces of this system: the sense that meaning resides as much in emphasis as in words themselves.

> **Mini-Essay — Stress as Syntax**
>
> In a line such as *“The things which I have seen I now can see no more”* (Wordsworth, *Ode: Intimations of Immortality*, 1807), stress falls not simply by grammatical rule but by emotional weight. The crescendo of accent mirrors the loss the line mourns. Metre, in this sense, is a **syntax of feeling**: a system by which emotion finds measurable form (Wordsworth 1807).

---

## Iambic Pentameter: Elastic Precision

The **iambic pentameter** — five feet of alternating unstressed and stressed syllables — became the **signature rhythm of English poetry**, adaptable, flexible, and capacious. Yet it is not mechanical. As the lecture notes emphasise, “Shakespeare’s writing is sometimes split into 6–4,” revealing an underlying rhythm that breathes and shifts (Dasgupta 2025).

> “So long as men can breathe, or eyes can see,”  
> — Shakespeare, *Sonnet 18* (Shakespeare 1609)

The line contains an **implied caesura**, a pause that breaks the measure without violating it. Metre thus enacts tension between **rule and freedom**, **pattern and deviation**. Poetic meaning often resides precisely in this **counterpoint** between sense and rhythm.

> **Mini-Essay — Metre as Counterpoint**
>
> Wordsworth observed that when reading blank verse, “it will be physically impossible to pronounce the last words or syllables of the line with the same indifference as the others.” The **“passion of metre”** forces a pause even when syntax urges continuation. This friction between syntactic flow and metrical closure generates poetry’s characteristic vitality — a pulse that never quite resolves, the sound of thought thinking against its own constraints (Wordsworth 1804).

---

## The Passion of Metre

Wordsworth’s 1804 letter on reading aloud speaks of two kinds of “passion” — that of the subject and that of the metre. The end of a verse line invites pause, not because sense demands it but because rhythm does. The **metrical beat imposes affect**; it turns mere syntax into experience.

This “passion of metre” is a phenomenon of perception: the way the ear feels form. Even when we no longer scan consciously, rhythm moves us. It carries what Wordsworth called the “still, sad music of humanity” — the measured trace of feeling rendered intelligible through recurrence.

> **Mini-Essay — The Double Passion**
>
> The line’s music does not simply echo emotion; it **creates it**. The reader’s breath, constrained by rhythm, enacts the poem’s drama. To read *“The world is too much with us; late and soon”* is to perform frustration in the very compression of stress. Metre becomes **an emotional machine**, generating sympathy through repetition (Wordsworth 1807).

---

## Metre and the Human Voice

Metre, as the lecture notes remind us, is never wholly distinct from natural speech. It evolves from it, yet stylises it — a form of **“measured speech.”** The most effective metres approximate the rhythms of spontaneous utterance. Martin Luther King’s “I have a dream” and the American Declaration’s “We hold these truths to be self-evident” both fall naturally into pentametric rhythm (Dasgupta 2025).

Such phrases demonstrate that metrical structure is **not alien to ordinary language** but latent within it. The repetition of rhythm establishes authority, solemnity, and emotional coherence. Metre here functions politically as well as aesthetically: it organises conviction.

> **Mini-Essay — Oratory and Poetry**
>
> King’s refrain, “I have a dream,” sets a beat that disciplines hope. Like the pentameter, it balances expectation and renewal — each iteration expanding the vision yet anchoring it in form. This affinity between **poetic metre and moral rhetoric** reveals metre’s civic dimension: rhythm as persuasion, cadence as communal heartbeat (Dasgupta 2025).

---

## The Fall of Metre: Free Verse and Modern Speech

By the early twentieth century, poets began to **abandon traditional metre**, embracing “free verse.” The phrase itself, as shown by digital corpus studies, scarcely existed before 1914; it then “shoots to prominence” (Dasgupta 2025). What had long been the norm became a mark of conservatism. To write in sonnets or strict measure could even, by the 1970s and 80s, be interpreted as politically reactionary — an allegiance to “Reaganism,” as critics jested.

Yet the impulse behind free verse was not a rejection of rhythm but a **redefinition of it**. Modern poets sought the “natural music” of speech, the rhythms of conversation and breath. In this sense, free verse continues the logic of Wordsworth’s blank verse — measured by the “real language of men.”

> **Mini-Essay — Freedom’s Measure**
>
> Free verse is not formless; it measures by **pulse, not pattern**. The ghost of metre persists even in its absence. Each irregular line testifies to an inherited discipline, an echo of constraint. As Meredith Martin argues, the “literary movements around the time of the First World War… erased a vast history of debates about versification.” To recover metre, then, is not antiquarianism but archaeology — uncovering the lost grammar of our listening (Martin 2012).

---

## Metre as Ethics: Form and Truth

Plato’s fear and Wordsworth’s faith both arise from the same insight: metre **shapes moral attention**. The regularity of rhythm trains perception; it models order, patience, and discipline. To read a sonnet attentively is to inhabit a pattern of thought. The alternation of stress and release mirrors the tension of moral life — the pull between impulse and restraint.

Victorian critics inherited this sense of moral rhythm. George Eliot, though writing primarily in prose, defined art as “the nearest thing to life.” The **measured language of poetry** embodied that ideal: form as empathy, artifice as moral attention.

> **Mini-Essay — The Ethics of Attention**
>
> To scan a poem is to listen ethically. Each beat demands awareness, each variation invites judgment. The discipline of metre cultivates what Simone Weil would later call *attente* — the patience of listening. In this way, poetic rhythm teaches an ethics of reading: to measure, to attend, to care (Eliot 1856).

---

## Historical Continuity: From Old English to Modern Speech

Even as pronunciation and stress patterns have changed, the instinct for **metrical order** remains. Old English alliterative verse, Chaucer’s iambs, Shakespeare’s pentameters, and modern oratory all trace the same human need: to give emotion a body through rhythm. Metre thus belongs neither to an era nor a school; it is a **cultural constant**, periodically rediscovered.

Some linguistic changes have rendered older metres less “natural” to modern ears, perhaps contributing to the rise of free verse. Yet our daily speech still pulses with metrical possibility. Nursery rhymes, advertising slogans, and protest chants retain the **ancestral beat** of verse: the rhythm of belonging and memory.

---

## Motif Tracker

| **Motif** | **Example(s)** | **Interpretive Use** |
|------------|----------------|----------------------|
| **Measure / Measurement** | “Metre means literally measured language”; *metron* | Symbol of order and intelligibility; connects linguistic form to ethical precision. |
| **Restraint / Constraint** | Dante’s *terza rima* as “metrical alarm” | Metre as moral discipline and textual incorruptibility. |
| **Voice / Speech** | “I have a dream”; “We hold these truths…” | Continuity between poetic metre and civic oratory; rhythm as persuasion. |
| **Memory / Transmission** | “Verse which is measured is always more easily remembered” (Aristotle) | Mnemonic and cultural continuity; poetry as collective archive. |
| **Passion of Metre** | Wordsworth’s pause at line-end | Rhythm as emotional syntax; metre produces feeling, not merely expresses it. |
| **Freedom / Deviation** | Shakespeare’s “6–4” line division | Tension between form and spontaneity; deviation as creative vitality. |
| **Silence / Pause** | Caesura, end-stop | The ethics of listening; space as rhythm’s counterpart. |

---

## Critical Lenses

| **Axis** | **Relationship** | **Interpretive Implication** |
|-----------|------------------|-------------------------------|
| **Form ↔ Feeling** | Metre transforms emotion into pattern | Rhythm is the bridge between passion and articulation. |
| **Sound ↔ Sense** | Words “trifle on the ear” yet convey truth | Aesthetic pleasure becomes moral persuasion. |
| **Freedom ↔ Restraint** | Poetic discipline versus expressive liberty | Art as negotiation between necessity and choice. |
| **Speech ↔ Writing** | Oral tradition in written form | Metre preserves orality within print culture. |
| **Memory ↔ Innovation** | Tradition as resource for modernity | Even free verse remembers its metrical ancestry. |
| **Philosophy ↔ Poetry** | Plato’s suspicion versus Wordsworth’s defence | Metre as battleground between truth and beauty. |

---

## Exam Relevance

### Connection to Oxford Prelims Papers

**Paper 1 (Introduction to English Language and Literature):**  
- Demonstrates the interplay of **linguistic structure and literary form** — how stress, pitch, and duration shape meaning.  
- Encourages precision in **close reading and scansion**, a skill transferable to all period papers.  
- Illuminates the relationship between **sound and syntax**, preparing students for stylistic commentary.

**Paper 3 (Literature in English 1830–1910):**  
- Contextualises the **ethical and emotional uses of metre** in Romantic and Victorian poetry (Wordsworth, Tennyson, Browning).  
- Shows how nineteenth-century writers inherit and question classical form.  
- Builds competence in linking **form, voice, and ideology** — key to essays on realism, lyric, or aesthetics.

### Core Analytical Skills

- Scansion and stress analysis as interpretive tools.  
- Relating **form to content**: metre as argument.  
- Evaluating **deviation and regularity** within poetic language.  
- Historicising formal choices — understanding metre as cultural practice.  

### Model Essay Patterns

1. **Form as Thought:** Explore how metrical tension enacts thematic conflict.  
2. **Metre and Meaning:** Compare poets who use form to reveal rather than conceal truth.  
3. **Historical Argument:** Trace the decline or transformation of metre across periods.  

### Revision Strategies

- Practise scansion across genres (lyric, blank verse, dramatic monologue).  
- Annotate for stress, caesura, and enjambment.  
- Compare metrical effects across centuries — from Chaucer to Yeats.  
- Rehearse oral reading: feel how rhythm governs sense.  

---

## Conclusion: The Measure of the Human

To speak of metre is to speak of **the human need for pattern** — to find measure within chaos, to make emotion audible. From Plato’s suspicion to Wordsworth’s reverence, from Dante’s rhyme-chains to King’s oratory, metre persists as a way of thinking through rhythm. It is both structure and surprise, rule and revelation. The poem measures language so that language may measure us.

> **Mini-Essay — The Enduring Beat**
>
> Even when poets declare themselves free of metre, they remain haunted by its ghost. Rhythm is the oldest inheritance of speech — our first music. To listen for it is to join a lineage that stretches from chant to sonnet to sermon. In learning to hear metre, we learn to hear ourselves.

---

## Reference List (MHRA Style)

Aristotle, *Poetics*, trans. Malcolm Heath (London: Penguin, 1996).  
Dante Alighieri, *The Divine Comedy*, trans. Dorothy L. Sayers (Harmondsworth: Penguin, 1949).  
Dasgupta, Ushashi, *Measured Language: Metre and Meaning in English Poetry* (Lecture, Oxford University, 2025).  
Eliot, George, ‘The Natural History of German Life’, *Westminster Review*, July 1856, pp. 51–79.  
Martin, Meredith, *The Rise and Fall of Meter: Poetry and English National Culture, 1860–1930* (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 2012).  
Plato, *Republic*, trans. Robin Waterfield (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 1993).  
Shakespeare, William, *Sonnets* (London: Thomas Thorpe, 1609).  
Sidney, Sir Philip, *An Apology for Poetry*, ed. Geoffrey Shepherd (Manchester: Manchester University Press, 2002).  
Wordsworth, William, *Ode: Intimations of Immortality* (1807) and *Letter to a Friend on Reading Aloud* (1804).  
Oxford University, *1-ELL Prelims Handbook 2025–26* (Oxford: Faculty of English, 2025).  
Clare, John, quoted in *Metre – PRE1,3W2 Lecture Notes* (Oxford University, 2025).

## what is stress
- added emphasis on particular syllables on a line
- old english poetry is based on patterns of stress/accents but also pitch changes and duration changes (same syllables can be stressed in modern and old english but could have been performed differently, different way of stressing words)

- stressed syllables have clear, but composite, acoustic correlates
	- length/duration (a phoneme is the smallest unit of speech distinguishing one word from another)
		- bit vs beat
	- intesity/loudness (not phonemic)
	- pitch/pitch change (not phonemic)

1. lexical stress on words
	- belonging to individual words (only words of more than one syllable)
	- two syllable words are usually trochaic when they are nouns (proper, common, compound)
		-  black*bird* vs *black* bird
		- green*house* vs *green* house
	- two syllable verbs have the opposite pattern
		- *pre*sent vs pre*sent*
		- *rec*ord vs rec*ord*
		- *tor*ment vs tor*ment*
	- ==> minimal pair: a pair of words that differ by only one sound (phoneme) but have different meanings
2. prosodic stress
	- produced by grouping words together into a compound, pjrase or sentence
	- this is the only way in which monosyllables become stressed
		- The *things* which *I* have *seen* I *now* can *see* no *more*
	- WHen we build up sentences, the stress clumps towards the end (nuclear stress rule), as more is added, the stress builds up and up

		- I went out to the Shops this morning to buy a Cake

	- once the crescendo breaks, new sentence/significant clause
	- focal stress: emphasis can alter the ordinary stress pattern of a phrase

# Cleaned Up Notes


## Metre III: Non-Iambic Metres and Rhyme  
### Ushashi Dasgupta — Oxford Prelims Paper 1 & 3 (2025)

---

## Introduction: Beyond the Iamb  

In previous lectures Dasgupta examined the iambic line as the foundation of English verse.  
This lecture turns to **non-iambic metres** — trochaic, dactylic, anapaestic — and to **rhyme** as their companion structure:contentReference[oaicite:0]{index=0}.  
The argument throughout is that metre and rhyme are not decorative but conceptual: they shape how meaning, grammar, and emotion move through time.

> **Mini-Essay — Why Other Metres Matter**  
> English verse has privileged the iamb because its two-beat alternation mirrors the rhythm of speech and heartbeat.  
> But, as Dasgupta notes, “non-iambic metres place greater constraints on expression,” forcing poets to invent new syntactic patterns to fit new rhythmic possibilities:contentReference[oaicite:1]{index=1}.  
> The difficulty of such metres becomes their expressive resource.

---

## I. Lines, Syntax, and the Question of the Line-End  

Jeremy Bentham once quipped: “When the lines run all the way to the right margin, it’s prose; when this fails to happen, it’s verse.”  
Yet in **metrical verse**, the line break is not arbitrary. It governs tone and thought.  
Dasgupta revisits **Wordsworth’s opening of *The Prelude*** — “Was it for this, that one / The fairest of all rivers…” — to show how a single word at a line-end (“this”) acquires existential weight by being isolated:contentReference[oaicite:2]{index=2}.

> **Mini-Essay — The Pause as Meaning**  
> Ending a line on “this” suspends reference. The demonstrative dangles between past and present, expectation and disappointment.  
> A metrical pause becomes a metaphysical question: *Was it for this ?*

In free verse, line breaks can be altered at will; in metrical verse, they are **structural**.  
As Dasgupta remarks, “A good lineation isn’t a transcription device but a moral architecture — an obligation to pause where meaning resists closure.”:contentReference[oaicite:3]{index=3}

---

## II. Free Verse and the Politics of Form  

Dasgupta contrasts the fixity of metre with the ideological claims of **free verse**.  
For **Ezra Pound**, rhythm should be “a man’s own — his own counterfeiting on counterfeit bills.”  
**Richard Aldington** called free verse “the cadence a poet creates for himself,” while **D. H. Lawrence** likened metrical regularity to “drill sergeants barking orders.”  

These poets justified formal freedom as **political freedom** — a rebellion against what Aldington called “childish formality.”  
Yet Dasgupta cautions that the binary between individuality and constraint is false: “metrical poetry already contains variation; free verse depends on inherited rhythm even when it denies it”:contentReference[oaicite:4]{index=4}.

> **Mini-Essay — Liberty and Law**  
> Every free-verse line is haunted by its iambic ancestry.  
> The poem that rejects metre must still answer its ghost — an act of rebellion that confirms what it resists.

---

## III. Trochaic and Dactylic Metres  

### Trochaic  

A **trochee** reverses the iamb: / stressed + unstressed /.  
Trochaic rhythm feels incantatory, suited to songs, imperatives, and spells.  
Dasgupta notes that in English it is **harder to sustain** because the language tends naturally to end on stressed syllables.  
Hence trochaic poems often truncate the final unstressed beat — becoming *catalectic*.  

Example: Shakespeare’s *“Fear no more the heat o’ the sun”* — a perfect demonstration of the trochaic tetrameter catalectic.  
Ending on a stress (“sun,” “done,” “wages”) produces firmness and elegy at once.

> **Mini-Essay — Falling Rhythm and Finality**  
> Trochaic metre feels like descent — the motion of grief, gravity, or chant.  
> Its catalectic endings enact loss formally: the missing syllable becomes the sound of absence.

### Dactylic and Anapaestic  

A **dactyl** ( / x x ) and **anapaest** ( x x / ) generate triple time — the “skipping beat.”  
Dasgupta calls them “the metres of motion, laughter, and lamentation,” but also “the hardest to sustain in English,” whose stress patterns are binary:contentReference[oaicite:5]{index=5}.  

**Shelley’s “To a Skylark”** exemplifies the problem: nominally dactylic, it continually shortens or overruns its pattern.  
Each stanza closes with a longer line (“…in profuse strains of unpremeditated art”) that breaks the rhythm to end the pursuit of the bird’s “ungraspable song.”  

**Longfellow’s *Song of Hiawatha*** (1855) demonstrates both the power and monotony of sustained trochaic tetrameter.  
Its hypnotic rhythm — “By the shore of Gitche Gumee, / By the shining Big-Sea-Water” — drew on Finnish sources but imposed a colonial fantasy of “Native” cadence.  
Modern critics, Dasgupta notes, read its repetitions as both **appropriation** and **memorial**: a rhythm that records what it silences:contentReference[oaicite:6]{index=6}.

> **Mini-Essay — Rhythm and Empire**  
> Longfellow’s metre tries to “honour” indigenous song yet standardises it into European symmetry.  
> Metre here is not neutral pattern but ideology: the beat of cultural translation.

---

## IV. The Imperative Metre: Donne and Auden  

**John Donne’s “Go and catch a falling star”** (c.1600) uses the same trochaic-dactylic compound pattern.  
Its rhythm, half-command, half-lullaby, sets impossible tasks:  

> “Go and catch a falling star, / Get with child a mandrake root…”  

Each stanza truncates its last line — again *catalectic* — producing what Dasgupta calls “open-ended sound suggestive of falling away.”:contentReference[oaicite:7]{index=7}  
The poem’s cynicism about constancy is softened by the meter’s sing-song charm.  

**W. H. Auden’s “Lullaby”** (1937) reworks the same pattern:  

> “Lay your sleeping head, my love, / Human on my faithless arm.”  

Dasgupta highlights the **chain of imperatives** (“lay,” “sleep,” “let”) that link Auden to Donne.  
Both use falling rhythm to voice tenderness shadowed by loss.  
The metre’s unresolved cadence mirrors erotic transience — what Dasgupta calls “temporal impoverishment”: every lullaby is also elegy:contentReference[oaicite:8]{index=8}.

> **Mini-Essay — Falling Rhythms, Falling Worlds**  
> Because the trochaic line ends unstressed, it always falls away.  
> Its motion figures impermanence — the erotic, the mortal, the incomplete.  
> Donne’s misogynist wit and Auden’s homoerotic tenderness share that formal melancholy.

---

## V. Dolnik, Nursery, and the Popular Beat  

Dasgupta introduces the Russian term **dolnik**, used by Derek Attridge to describe accentual verse that counts stresses rather than syllables.  
English nursery rhymes — “Doctor Foster went to Gloucester” — fall into this flexible pattern.  
Victorian and modern poets alike mined this rhythm for its mingled innocence and irony.  

**Tennyson’s “Locksley Hall”** (1842) stretches the metre into expansive, almost cinematic rhythm:  

> “Comrades, leave me here a little, / While as yet is early morn;  
> Leave me here, and when you want me, / Sound upon the bugle-horn.”  

Dasgupta calls this a “four-beat gallop” that oscillates between marching song and lament:contentReference[oaicite:9]{index=9}.  
Its long lines verge on spoken prose, foreshadowing the **metrical instability** of the late nineteenth century.  

**Elizabeth Barrett Browning’s “The Cry of the Children”** (1843) mixes binary and triple metres to embody exhaustion — the rhythm of industrial labour itself.  

> **Mini-Essay — Rhythm and History**  
> The move from steady iamb to uneven dolnik mirrors industrial dislocation: poetic rhythm registers the tempo of modern life.  
> The “nearly regular” beat becomes the sound of a society out of step.

---

## VI. Dactylic Memory: Hardy’s “The Voice”  

In **Thomas Hardy’s “The Voice”** (1912) the dactylic rhythm turns elegy into echo:  

> “Woman much missed, how you call to me, call to me…”  

Each line drops two syllables at the close, leaving a hollow cadence.  
The missing beats become the sound of absence itself — “as if the poem listens into its own silence,” Dasgupta writes:contentReference[oaicite:10]{index=10}.  

**Seamus Heaney**, in *Poems That Make Grown Men Cry* (2014), singled out Hardy’s last stanza as almost physically painful:  
the line “Can it be you that I hear?” breaks metre at the instant of recognition and doubt.  

> **Mini-Essay — Meter as Mourning**  
> Hardy’s dactyls translate grief into breath: the regular pulse falters, mimicking the body’s attempt to call the lost.  
> Metre becomes memory’s measure — the beat that persists when words fail.

---

## VII. Rhyme: Sound, Expectation, and Freedom  

### Perfect and Partial Rhyme  

Dasgupta turns from rhythm to **rhyme** as “the logic of recurrence through difference.”  
Linguistically, rhyme joins two syllables with distinct onsets but identical nuclei + codas (“cat”/“mat”).  
Yet most poets exploit **partial rhyme** — consonance, assonance, eye-rhyme — to vary predictability.  

In *In Memoriam*, Tennyson’s perfect rhymes (“must/dust,” “shade/made”) express faith in order.  
By contrast, **modern poets prefer imperfection**, what Dasgupta calls “return grounded in difference”:contentReference[oaicite:11]{index=11}.  

### The Decline of the Perfect Rhyme  

Milton had already condemned rhyme as “the troublesome and modern bondage” (*Preface to Paradise Lost*, 1667).  
By the twentieth century, perfect rhyme felt mechanical.  
Dasgupta cites Larkin’s miniature masterpiece *“As Bad as a Mile”*:  

> “Watching the shied core strike the basket, / Skidding across the floor…”  

Its **half-rhymes** (“basket”/“back at”) turn control into entropy — rhyme as the sound of order unravelling.  

> **Mini-Essay — Predictability and Surprise**  
> Perfect rhyme gratifies; imperfect rhyme unsettles.  
> The reader’s half-met expectation becomes an analogue for modern doubt: knowledge that pattern exists, but cannot be trusted.

---

## VIII. The Gender of Rhyme  

“Masculine” rhymes end on stress (*stand/land*); “feminine” on an unstressed syllable (*feather/weather*).  
Critics once echoed Milton in calling rhyme effeminate — “petticoats for Achilles.”  
Dasgupta notes the persistence of this gendered metaphor: formal looseness equated with moral weakness.  

> **Mini-Essay — Rhyme and Gendered Labour**  
> Feminine rhyme, with its surplus syllable, performs excess — the decorative “extra” that patriarchy devalues.  
> Yet poets from Byron to Auden reclaim it as wit, proving that ornament can outthink austerity.

---

## IX. From Pope to Auden: Rhyme as Argument  

In heroic couplets, **Pope’s “reasonable rhymes”** create logical closure:  

> “Where’er you walk, cool gales shall fan the glade,  
> Trees where you sit shall crowd into a shade.”  

Kenneth Cochrane observed that such rhymes feel “unnaturally reasonable” — too complete, too symmetrical.  
Modern poets disrupt that completion.  

Auden’s *New Year Letter* (1940) and *“The Fugue”* turn rhyme into dialectic:  

> “Art in intention is mimesis,  
> But realised, the resemblance ceases.  
> Art is not life and cannot be  
> A midwife to society;  
> For art is a fait accompli.”  

The final French phrase (“fait accompli”) over-rhymes “be/society,” making rhyme itself foreign, ironic, performative.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Wit of Excess**  
> Polysyllabic rhyme, like Auden’s “society/accompli,” flirts with bad taste.  
> It shows rhyme’s evolution from moral closure to intellectual play — a structure for thinking rather than agreeing.

---

## Conclusion: Constraint and Invention  

Dasgupta ends by reframing metre and rhyme not as restrictions but as **creative conditions**.  
Each pattern — iambic, trochaic, dactylic — carries its own epistemology, a way of feeling time and truth.  

> **Mini-Essay — The Ethics of Pattern**  
> “To write in metre,” Dasgupta concludes, “is to accept rhythm as responsibility.”  
> The poet obeys the past only to discover new motion within it.  
> Freedom, paradoxically, depends on form.

---

## Motif Tracker  

| **Motif** | **Example(s)** | **Interpretive Use** |
|------------|----------------|----------------------|
| **Falling Rhythm / Loss** | Donne’s “Go and catch a falling star”; Auden’s “Lullaby”; Hardy’s “The Voice” | Trochaic and dactylic metres as figures of descent; rhythm mimics mortality and desire. |
| **Freedom and Constraint** | Pound’s free verse; Tennyson’s iambics; Hardy’s defence of metre | Formal pattern as a paradox of liberty: freedom discovered through obedience to structure. |
| **Rhyme and Return** | Tennyson’s *In Memoriam*; Larkin’s half-rhymes | Rhyme as temporal recurrence; faith and doubt staged as sound expectation and disruption. |
| **Gender and Form** | “Masculine” and “feminine” rhymes; Byron and Auden | Poetic gendering of rhyme exposes cultural hierarchies; excess reclaimed as intellectual play. |
| **History in Rhythm** | Longfellow’s *Song of Hiawatha*; Barrett Browning’s “The Cry of the Children” | Metre as ideological marker; rhythm records colonial and industrial tempos. |
| **Imperative and Intimacy** | Donne’s commands; Auden’s lullaby | Trochaic imperatives express both mastery and tenderness; rhythm as relation. |
| **Sound and Silence** | Hardy’s truncations; dactylic omissions | The missing beat as formal absence; silence rendered audible. |
| **Ethics of Pattern** | Dasgupta’s claim that metre is “responsibility” | Rhythm as moral attention: the poet’s discipline mirrors ethical awareness. |

---

## Critical Lenses  

| **Axis** | **Relationship** | **Implication** |
|-----------|------------------|----------------|
| **Metre ↔ Meaning** | Form generates emotion and knowledge | Rhythm not as vessel but as argument; time itself becomes theme. |
| **Pattern ↔ Deviation** | Variation within rule | Expression found in breaking expectation; beauty as controlled disobedience. |
| **Colonialism ↔ Aesthetics** | Longfellow’s imitation of “Native” chant | Metre as instrument of cultural power and appropriation. |
| **Gender ↔ Ornament** | Feminine rhyme and “decoration” | Revaluation of surplus syllable as wit, not weakness. |
| **Freedom ↔ Inheritance** | Free verse versus traditional metre | Every innovation haunted by its formal ancestry; rebellion validates the rule. |
| **Memory ↔ Sound** | Hardy’s elegiac dactyls | Metre as temporal persistence; grief measured by recurrence. |
| **Constraint ↔ Responsibility** | Dasgupta’s closing statement | Poetic discipline as moral stance: form teaches care and patience. |

---

## Exam Relevance  

### Core Relevance to Prelims Papers 1 and 3  

- Demonstrates deep formal analysis for close-reading papers: **metrical scanning, rhyme schemes, and lineation**.  
- Links **metrical variation** to historical, political, and emotional meanings.  
- Builds bridges between **early and modern verse traditions**, showing continuity of experiment.  
- Encourages discussion of **form as ethics** — how restraint produces originality.  

### Analytical Skills  

- Performing scansion and relating rhythm to tone or syntax.  
- Recognising how **metre encodes ideology** — colonial, gendered, or aesthetic.  
- Describing **rhyme types** (perfect, partial, eye, assonance) with interpretive precision.  
- Using **comparative reading** to link Donne, Hardy, Auden, and free-verse modernists.  

### Model Essay Patterns  

1. **“Falling rhythms express both tenderness and loss.”** — Compare Donne, Auden, and Hardy.  
2. **“Freedom is found in obedience.”** — Discuss the paradox of constraint in metrical form.  
3. **“Rhyme is the sound of history repeating itself.”** — Trace rhyme’s evolution from Tennyson to Larkin.  
4. **“Every metre carries its own morality.”** — Explore Dasgupta’s claim that form is ethical attention.  

### Revision Strategies  

- Practise scanning lines of different metres aloud — trochaic, dactylic, anapaestic.  
- Identify **catalexis** (truncated line-endings) and how they affect tone.  
- Compare poets’ uses of imperfect rhyme: how modern dissonance revises Victorian order.  
- Review poems where **metre and subject diverge** (e.g., playful rhythm with tragic content).  

---

Attridge, Derek, *Poetic Rhythm: An Introduction* (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1995).

forms of verse
